## The complete source progression and depth

This map records what the supplied excerpt does in order. It is not an assertion that every future lesson should cover every listed topic.

### 1. The relationship comes before its parts

**The Art of the If/Then**, HTML lines 142 to 154, opens with a plain definition, a single familiar rule, a literal interpretation, several statements of certainty, and a reason to understand the relationship beneath varied language. The transition promises to take the same example apart.

The depth comes from making “must” meaningful before presenting a list of words. The reader learns what kind of relationship they are looking for. The source also connects certainty to prior “Power Players” material, which is not included in this attachment. Our standalone introduction must supply any needed context itself.

**Transfer:** first explain what a conditional says and why its direction matters. Then give a concrete example. Do not open with a diagramming task or make the definition a label attached after a quiz.

### 2. Sufficient is explained through presence and absence

**The Sufficient Condition**, lines 156 to 199, contains a definition, the recurring anchor, a metaphor, the possibility that the sufficient condition does not occur, a contrast between its absence and presence, and a clarification of “activation.” It then shows **eight indicator rows with eleven displayed example forms**, all expressing the same anchor relationship, and explains why language recognition cannot replace understanding.

This is more than “sufficient goes left.” The text develops what the condition does, what it does not establish, and how wording changes around it.

**Transfer:** give sufficient its own explanation, contrasting cases, and nearby practice. A left-side label is useful only after “enough for what?” has a clear answer.

### 3. Necessary is developed as a requirement

**The Necessary Condition**, lines 201 to 230, defines the other part, reuses the same rule, explains the requirement, and shows **ten indicator rows with eleven displayed forms**. It compares the varied wording with the sufficient-family statements and gives special attention to “only if.” It ends by explaining why symbols help isolate the relationship.

The source uses the ordinary force of “necessary,” “required,” and “need” to make the technical term familiar. It does not jump directly to a memorized side rule.

**Transfer:** explain what is required and why having a requirement is not the same as satisfying every condition for the result. Teach “if” and “only if” using a controlled comparison. Keep the target of each expression clear.

### 4. The contrapositive is discovered before it is manipulated

**The Contrapositive**, lines 234 to 285, first recovers the original relationship. It introduces a new coffee/walking thought experiment, removes the required condition, asks for a prediction, and explicitly pauses. The answer is derived from the unmet requirement. The term is then named, the original anchor revisited, and a four-situation chart sets limits on inference.

The order is important: meaning, prediction, reason, name, comparison. The flip-and-negate shortcut appears later, after the reader has a reason to believe the result.

**Transfer:** preserve this order when contraposition is actually in scope. Do not rush the full procedure into the opening definition merely because it appears early in this source.

### 5. Notation is introduced as a solution to varied wording

**How to Diagram Conditionals**, lines 289 to 388, recovers the indicator families, explains the arrow, gives a four-step construction procedure, and builds one diagram in stages. It then explains negation, displays four original/contrapositive sign patterns, gives the shortcut, and distinguishes writing a contrapositive from reading one when needed.

The first diagram is narrated: notice the indicator, place its target, then complete the other side. Its final appearance is not used as evidence that the learner understands how to make it.
## What to carry into the Conditional Bootcamp

**Joshua's instruction on 2026-10-03 changes the conditional curriculum scope and sequencing.** The complete supplied HTML now sets its content-order backbone, as well as its voice: if/then meaning; sufficient and its word family; necessary and its word family; conceptual contrapositive; four situations; diagramming and symbolic contraposition; practical FAQ; indicator targets; Personalized If/Then; integrated basic practice. Preserve every supplied concept in that relative order, with original examples and additional depth. The [complete ordered skeleton and integration map](../brainstorms/2026-09-30-conditional-curriculum/2026-10-02-opening-lessons-source-map.md) is the current authoring plan.

This explicitly includes a conceptual contrapositive before notation and supersedes the earlier restriction to the first four opening units. Existing code does not become sequence-compliant by being linked to this plan. Keep the source's logical simplifications bounded, preserve Joshua's five-step method and contextual group definitions, and preserve assignment and historical-answer compatibility when implementing the sequence. This source-specific curriculum order does not impose conditional topics on unrelated LSAT lessons.

The following rows record the earlier opening-only planning model and useful concept/practice coverage. Their arrangement is **not the current teaching sequence**. Reuse their material in the new ordered skeleton rather than retaining their old lesson order.

| Part of our opening | What the learner should understand | Natural teaching progression | Nearby practice |
|---|---|---|---|
| **What a conditional says** | A statement makes one condition enough to require another. It does not establish that either condition occurs. | Plain definition, purpose, one stated rule, one case, one tempting reverse. | Decide what the given rule and fact establish; explain the limit. |
| **Why it matters on the LSAT** | A conclusion can rely on the direction of a rule or on a missing connection. Reversing a relationship can support the wrong answer. | Keep the beginner example; show one supported conclusion and one unsupported inference. | Choose the justified conclusion and name the connection the other answer would require. |
| **Sufficient** | Enough to establish the necessary condition under this rule; not the only possible way unless stated. | Same anchor, “enough for what?”, present-case explanation, absence leaves the other condition open. | Find the complete sufficient idea and say what it establishes. |
| **Necessary** | Required for the sufficient condition; presence alone does not establish the sufficient condition. | Same anchor, identify what must be there, compare requirement with guarantee. | Identify the requirement; reject an unsupported reverse with a reason. |
| **Translation and diagramming** | The diagram preserves the whole relationship, including conditions and qualifications. | Ordinary meaning, indicator, complete ideas, placement, English readback. Introduce later operations when their prerequisites are taught. | Construct a diagram in meaningful steps and compare it with the whole sentence. |
| **Group 1** | Joshua's Group 1 expressions identify the sufficient idea; sentence order can vary. | Teach a simple expression first, reuse meaning across variants, then alter position and scope. | Lesson, guided translation, fresh independent items, explained feedback. |
| **Group 2** | Joshua's Group 2 expressions identify the necessary idea; target and force must be read in context. | Teach the requirement first, compare “if” with “only if,” then develop each expression's target and nuances. | Separate Group 2 practice, then a controlled Group 1/2 comparison when both are learned. |

These are curriculum planning rows. Student-facing headings should teach the actual concept, such as **What does this sentence require?** They should not expose “Joshua's assigned starting stage,” internal sequencing metadata, or a difficulty selector. Joshua assigns the appropriate material; the learner works through that assigned lesson and its practice.

### Give an idea several teachable moments

The source rarely handles a significant concept with one definition and one answer. It may need a literal explanation, a familiar example, a variation, a pause, a mistaken interpretation, a correction, and a short practice task.

For Group 1, that could become several small lessons: recognizing “if,” locating the complete if-clause, reading an if-clause at the end of the sentence, handling a universal expression, and checking that the diagram preserves the meaning. Group 2 deserves its own development. Do not flatten all expressions into a word table and assume coverage.

Keep micro-assignments possible. A student who needs “only if” should be able to receive that work without repeating every beginner concept. A general assignment can connect the lessons into a full sequence. Beginner, medium, and advanced describe teacher-assigned work; optional timing belongs to medium and advanced practice after accuracy is established.

### Preserve the real indicator groups

The current reviewed opening materials use:

- **Group 1:** if, when, where, all, the only, every, any.
- **Group 2:** only, only if, only when, only where, always, requires, must.

See the [reviewed opening source constraints](../brainstorms/2026-09-30-conditional-curriculum/notebooklm-opening-assets/sufficient-necessary-infographic-prompt.txt). These lists are current project teaching groups, not a claim that every occurrence of each word mechanically diagrammed in isolation has the same function. The supplied source's two families have different members. Do not merge inventories without checking Joshua's notes and scope.

“The only” especially requires its complete grammatical target. The only people admitted are members means **Admitted → Member**. Only members are admitted means the same relationship, reached through a different expression. The lesson must show the meaning rather than encourage a next-word shortcut.

### Preserve Joshua's five steps

In [Sandra's 2026-09-28 transcript](../students/sandra/transcript-downloads/2026-09-28-sandra-notes-by-gemini.md), at 00:43:22, Joshua gives this sequence: identify the indicator; label the two ideas; apply the left/right rule; find the contrapositive; say it back in English. This is a summary of his sequence, not a verbatim quotation. At 00:37:08, he stresses understanding and reading the diagram rather than retaining bare letters.

The supplied source has four construction steps. Do not quietly replace Joshua's five with those four, or claim a shortened opening exercise has taught all five. In a gradual introduction, teach the operations whose prerequisites have been established and identify the full contrapositive step when that lesson is reached. Keep English readback visible as a meaning check throughout.

### Carry these improvements forward

1. **Define before demonstrating.** Start by explaining what a conditional says. Let the example make the definition concrete.
2. **Explain the purpose through a decision.** Show how direction changes the conclusion the learner may draw. Avoid unsupported prevalence statistics.
3. **Keep one relationship stable while changing the view.** Ordinary sentence, case fact, roles, wording variation, and diagram should fit together.
4. **Make each pause a useful thinking task.** Ask for a prediction or explanation, then reveal the reason.
5. **Show a mistake being detected.** A wrong arrow becomes understandable when its English meaning fails the original sentence.
6. **Teach the target before the placement shortcut becomes a habit.** Complete ideas and their qualifications matter.
7. **Explain why the drill exists.** The learner should know what ability they are practicing.
8. **Use fresh practice.** Worked examples teach the method; new items show whether the method transfers.
9. **Give practical objections an answer.** Time, confidence, notation, and when to write deserve specific guidance.
10. **Keep each operation behind its prerequisites.** Under the 2026-10-03 sequence, conceptual contraposition follows both condition roles and families, and precedes notation. Chains, Groups 3/4 and quantities still receive later development. The source does not contain those complete later curricula.

## Accuracy and method boundaries
## Use this rubric before handing over LSAT writing

Score each dimension from 0 to 2: **0** absent or misleading; **1** partly present but needing revision; **2** clear and usable. This is an editorial tool, not a validated learning measure. A logical error or scope violation requires revision regardless of the total.

| Dimension | What a strong draft does |
|---|---|
| **Ordinary meaning** | Defines the idea in language the reader can understand before notation. |
| **Concrete anchor** | Uses an original, small, unambiguous situation and states needed assumptions. |
| **Visible reasoning** | Shows the intermediate connection instead of asserting a result. |
| **Point of view** | Keeps the reader involved; uses teacher narration purposefully. |
| **Cadence** | Gives complex thoughts room and short conclusions a clear place to land. |
| **Vocabulary** | Keeps useful technical terms, explains them, and avoids internal jargon. |
| **Questions and pauses** | Asks a genuine decision and gives an appropriate chance to answer. |
| **Boundaries** | Shows what the information leaves open and why a tempting move fails. |
| **Progression** | Each operation has a prerequisite, a reason to appear, and adequate practice. |
| **Transfer** | Fresh questions require the taught skill, with reasoned feedback. |
| **Tone and warmth** | Expects effort, supports the next action, and never mocks uncertainty. |
| **Presentation** | Examples, comparisons, notation, and interactions clarify the reasoning. |

Do not treat a high total as proof of an identical voice. Use the original source and Joshua's response to calibrate the subjective feel. His concrete corrections override a score.

function reference(number: number): ContentSection {
  const section = sections(conditionalReferenceContent().props.children).find(item => item.props.number === number);
  if (!section) throw new Error(`Missing approved conditional reference section ${number}`);
  return section;
}

function groupSections(group: 'group-1' | 'group-2') {
  return sections(GroupLessons({ id: group }).props.children);
}

function groupSection(group: 'group-1' | 'group-2', title: string) {
  const section = groupSections(group).find(item => item.props.id === title || item.props.title === title);
  if (!section) throw new Error(`Missing approved conditional section: ${title}`);
  const sectionId = `course-${group}-${title.toLowerCase().replace(/[^a-z0-9]+/g, '-')}`;
  return cloneElement(section, { key: sectionId, id: sectionId });
}

function NoteSection({ title, children }: { title: string; children: ReactNode }) {
  return <section className="co-section"><div className="co-section-heading"><h2>{title}</h2></div>{children}</section>;
  return cloneElement(section, { key: sectionId, id: sectionId });
}

function NoteSection({ title, children }: { title: string; children: ReactNode }) {
  return <section className="co-section"><div className="co-section-heading"><h2>{title}</h2></div>{children}</section>;
}

Source element: <main>
---
Must arrow

What the arrow says, where to start reading it, and what it is for.

What the arrow says

An arrow joins the two conditions in a rule. It says one thing: if the condition on the left is true, the condition on the right must be true.

Read it as a sentence: “If you are enrolled in law school, then you must be a student.”
LEFT SIDE
Sufficient condition
You are enrolled in law school
Enough to guarantee the right side
must
RIGHT SIDE
Necessary condition
You are a student
Required when the left side is true

Being enrolled in law school is enough to know you are a student. That is why enrollment goes on the left. Being a student is required for enrollment, so it goes on the right. The arrow points from what is enough to what is required.

Start at the left

A rule begins where the arrow begins. Say “if” and read the left condition. Then say “then” and read the right one: If you are enrolled in law school, then you are a student.

Reading from the right says something else. “You are a student, so you are enrolled” is not what this rule says. The arrow only runs one way, and you start at its tail.

What the arrow is for

A rule can take a full sentence to say. The arrow shows what it promises in one line, so you can test a fact against it without rereading the sentence.

You learn the left side is true. The right side must be true too.
You learn the right side is true. The left side stays open.

This matters on the LSAT because arguments turn on which way a rule runs. A conclusion that follows the arrow is supported. A conclusion that runs against it needs something the rule never gave you.

Place the conditions. Then read the arrow.

Choose what belongs on each side, then use the same direction with a new rule.

Put the condition beside the arrow, not the indicator word. For example, write “you win the raffle,” not “if.”

0 answered · 4 remaining
Question 1 of 4

If you are enrolled in law school, then you are a student.

Which complete condition belongs on the left side of the arrow?
You are enrolled in law school.
You are a student.
If.
Check answer
Previous question
Next question →
Two ways to say the same thing

If you win the raffle, you get a bicycle.

You get a bicycle if you win the raffle.

Both mean: You win the raffle → You get a bicycle. Winning is sufficient, so it goes left, even when it comes last in the rule.

The arrow guarantees the right side when the left side is true. Being a student alone does not prove that you are enrolled in law school.

Why can't we reverse it?
PREVIOUS LESSON
The Necessary Condition
NEXT LESSON
The contrapositive

I rewrote the Must arrow note and added a contrapositive explainer, and pushed both as `fd3049a7`. I checked both pages in the preview. All 337 conditionals tests pass and typecheck is clean.

**Must arrow**
- **What the arrow says:** an arrow joins the two conditions in a rule, and it means "if the left is true, the right must be true." The example is read aloud as a sentence.
- **Start at the left:** you say "if" at the left condition and "then" at the right. Reading from the right says something the rule never said.
- **What it is for:** it shows what a rule promises in one line. A true left side forces the right side, and a true right side leaves the left open. The note also says why direction matters on the LSAT.
- **Kept as before:** the placement practice, the "two ways to say it" section, and the "why can't we reverse it" drop-down.
- **Lesson description:** now "What the arrow says, where to start reading it, and what it is for."

**Contrapositive**
- **Where:** it opens the main contrapositive lesson, before the sign patterns, as a full explainer with four headings.
- **What it is:** a raffle example, "If you do not get a bicycle, you do not win."
- **What it means:** it is the same rule read from the other end, shown as a pair of diagrams.
- **How to get it:** swap the sides, then negate both. It names the reverse and the inverse as the two wrong halves.
- **Why it matters:** a guaranteed conclusion next to a look-alike that is not guaranteed.
- **Check question:** one question at the end.

**What I did not change**
- **Contradictory lines:** the later part of that lesson still says you "needn't copy" the contrapositive and asks "Should I always write it?". Those lines sit in shared reference text that a parity test checks, so editing them means regenerating the content bundle. I'd do that as a separate step.
- **Compound conditions:** "If A and B, then C" is still not covered.
- **LSAT-style example:** there is none beyond the short Priya argument.

I wrote all the wording myself, so please read both pages.

# Write LSAT material in Joshua's selected teaching voice

Resolve the canonical ai-os vault from the workspace instructions. This project-owned skill takes precedence over older vendor skills with the same name that prescribe a writer blend.

Before drafting or revising, read:

1. [Canonical teaching voice](../../../wiki/teaching-voice.md), especially the teaching-content register.
2. [LSAT teaching voice standard](../../../references/lsat-teaching-voice-standard.md). Read it in full when calibrating the voice; for subsequent revisions, revisit the relevant sections and examples.
3. [Demonstrated copy corrections](../../../wiki/COPY-CORRECTIONS.md).

The detailed standard is maintained in one place. Do not copy it into this skill or replace it with generic brevity rules, required catchphrases, humor quotas, or a fixed number of sentences per paragraph.

Establish the actual lesson topic, prerequisites, evidence, and Joshua's approved method. The conditional reference teaches how to explain; it does not add conditional content to another topic. Preserve complete ideas, logical strength, modality, and the selected scope.

Use original intuitive examples. Explain the ordinary meaning before notation, show the intermediate reasoning, and give questions a real instructional job. Keep the teacher and reader thinking together. When useful, show a tempting interpretation and how its meaning fails. Fresh practice should require the taught skill and provide reasoned feedback.

Read the draft aloud, check its inferences and boundaries, and apply the standard's review rubric. Review actual prompt/reveal states when writing for an interactive surface. Do not claim an identical voice has been objectively established; use Joshua's corrections to refine calibration.

This skill governs local authoring. Posting, sending, assigning, deploying, or generating paid media requires the authorization and workflow appropriate to that action.

## How the teacher relates to the reader

### The reader is beside the teacher

The narrator talks directly to someone doing the work. “Let's,” “you,” and “we” keep the reader involved. The writing seldom steps back to describe what students generally do. It asks this reader to notice, try, decide, or check something now.

The reader is treated as capable of understanding ordinary meaning. The teacher supplies support when a technical step is new. In the opening, the source first gives the literal relationship, then asks the reader to take its certainty seriously. Later, it encourages a guess before revealing the contrapositive. These are expectations of participation, not claims that everything should feel easy.

**For our writing:** speak to the reader without making their intelligence the subject. Do not copy a line such as “Because you're a thinking human.” Give them a manageable question and the information to answer it. If they miss it, explain the precise move that went wrong.

### Confidence lives in the reasoning

The source is decisive about a valid consequence. It also says “We don't know” when the information cannot settle a question. These two kinds of confidence belong together. Clear teaching can be certain about the limits of what is known.

The teacher does not apologize for asking the reader to think. The explanation makes the request reasonable. In the FAQ, the narrator acknowledges early mistakes and explains the value of practicing anyway. The tone combines patience with a specific demand: attempt the question, write the diagram, inspect the error.

**For our writing:** state the consequence plainly and show why. Preserve legitimate uncertainty. Avoid changing a logical “could” into “must” to make the prose sound stronger. Avoid guarantees about learning outcomes, timing, or score changes unless we have evidence.

### Warmth points at the work

Encouragement is usually tied to an action. The reader can look back at the lesson, make a provisional attempt, learn from a wrong translation, or build confidence by checking work. The warmth comes from useful help and a teacher who expects progress.

Useful form:

> If the direction felt unclear, read the statement back in ordinary English. What does it say you need? Keep that complete idea in your diagram.

Weak form:

> You've got this! Conditionals are easy once you know the trick!

The weak version offers reassurance without a way forward and minimizes the actual difficulty.

### “I,” “we,” and “you” have different jobs

| Pronoun | What it does in the source | Use in our material |
|---|---|---|
| **You** | Gives the reader an action or asks for a decision. | “Try the sentence in the other direction. Does it still say the same thing?” |
| **We / let's** | Makes the explanation a shared investigation. | “Let's keep the rule and change the fact.” |
| **I**, thinking aloud | Shows the teacher making a decision: noticing an indicator, identifying its target, placing a label. | “I see ‘requires.’ What does it say is required?” |
| **I**, hypothetical | Places the speaker inside a condition. | “If I'm taking this course, what does the sentence say I need?” |
| **I**, personal judgment | Gives a recommendation or reports experience. | Use only a genuine Joshua judgment or verified experience. Do not borrow the source narrator's student history. |

Keep these roles stable. Do not rotate pronouns for cosmetic variety. In a platform instruction, “you” may be enough. In a teacher-authored walkthrough, “I” can make a decision visible. Marketing remains governed by its separate platform voice.

## Sentence length and spoken cadence

### Short sentences settle; longer sentences explain

The source does not make every sentence short. A longer sentence introduces a situation, explains motivation, or qualifies advice. Short sentences supply a result, mark a limit, or redirect attention.

The opening at HTML line 144 moves from a definition to a plain rephrasing and then the two-word landing “That's it.” Later, the source uses several brief sentences to think through a missing requirement, then states the consequence. The broad-purpose paragraph at line 152 is substantially longer. Its job is to explain why understanding the relationship matters across varied wording.

**For our writing:** let each sentence carry a manageable thought. A difficult inference may need several sentences. Do not trim away the intermediate reason just to produce a punchy conclusion. A short landing belongs after the explanation has earned it.

Original rhythm:

> The inspection stamp tells us the parcel was inspected. It doesn't tell us which sticker the shop gave it afterward. The rule hasn't answered that question.

The first sentence identifies what is known. The second identifies the missing connection. The third closes the uncertainty.

### The observed numbers are calibration, not quotas

An [approximate prose analysis](../raw/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice-standard/analysis-metrics.json) selects unclassified HTML paragraph elements, excluding the drill and obscured-line marker. It includes prose inside notes and FAQ answers, but excludes displayed examples, table cells, headings, and many short callouts. Words are alphanumeric tokens with internal apostrophes; sentences are split after `.`, `!`, or `?` followed by whitespace. This is a reproducible rough measure, not a linguistic parser.

| Measure in that subset | Observation |
|---|---:|
| Prose paragraphs | 73 |
| Prose words | 2,743 |
| Approximate sentences | 223 |
| Median sentence length | 10 words |
| Mean sentence length | 12.3 words |
| Sentence range | 1 to 53 words |
| Sentences of 8 words or fewer | 76 |
| Sentences over 25 words | 15 |
| Median paragraph length | 33 words |
| Mean paragraph length | 37.6 words |
| Paragraph range | 4 to 112 words |

There are 19 one-sentence and 16 two-sentence paragraphs in this subset. There are also paragraphs with seven, eight, and nine approximate sentences. This directly argues against making “three to five sentences per paragraph” a universal rule. The noticeable feature is variation matched to purpose, with space around a new task or example.

Read the draft aloud. Listen for three failures: several dense ideas in one breath; a string of clipped statements that never connect; a recurring catchphrase that sounds inserted. Fix the reasoning and breath before counting words.

### Contractions are normal; expansion creates contrast

The source uses contractions throughout: I'm, you're, we've, we'll, there's, doesn't, don't, can't, couldn't, isn't, and what's. This includes serious explanation and procedure, not only playful asides.

It sometimes expands a form for emphasis. “If I **am** breathing” makes the presence of the sufficient condition audible. We can use the same mechanism with new language:

> You don't know whether the parcel has a sticker. You **do** know it was inspected.

Do not mechanically contract every phrase. Keep a fuller form when it makes the distinction easier to hear.

### Punctuation follows the voice

Use full stops for changes in thought, colons before an example or sequence, and questions when a decision is genuinely at issue. Parentheses can hold a brief aside or notation clarification. Fragments are occasional conversational landings, not mandatory decorations.

Joshua's no-em-dash rule governs our writing. The source's occasional stretched spelling, all caps, ellipses, or exclamations are observations, not a punctuation recipe. Repeated “Awesome!”, “Cool,” “That's it,” or “NO!” would exaggerate the surface while weakening the teaching.

## Word choice and useful verbal habits

### Ordinary language carries the formal idea

The source retains sufficient condition, necessary condition, contrapositive, indicator, variable, and stimulus. It gives these words a familiar handle and shows what they mean. It does not hide the vocabulary the student will need.

The conversational words are often functional: “have to,” “need,” “what happens,” “what do you know,” “the other side,” “what's left,” “this fits.” They describe a move the reader can make.

| Word or phrase function | Source tendency | Writing instruction |
|---|---|---|
| Certainty | must, has to, necessary, no way around | Preserve the exact logical strength, then demonstrate it. |
| Possibility | might, could, may, we don't know | Say what remains open and why the rule leaves it open. |
| Drawing a consequence | so, that means, that implies, this tells me | Put the actual reason immediately before the consequence. |
| Giving a reason | because, since | Explain the matching relationship, not merely “because that's the rule.” |
| Inviting attention | let's look, notice, check, imagine | Name the thing to inspect or the fact to change. |
| Naming a boundary | but, that said, sometimes, unless | Keep the qualification beside the advice it limits. |
| Recovering context | so far, return to, now that | Remind the reader of the specific idea the new step depends on. |
| Testing an interpretation | this fits, does that follow, what would I need | Compare the proposed meaning with the whole original sentence. |

Do not replace natural speech with “identify the guaranteed outcome,” “activate the sufficient trigger,” or “execute the conditional reasoning procedure” when the learner needs an explanation. Technical wording earns its place by helping them think.

Joshua's browser corrections, 2026-10-03: define “guarantee” as the certainty supplied by the stated rule before using it to explain another idea. Explain “direction” through an actual relationship before using the word as advice. A beginner needs to see what the arrow means, which complete idea belongs on each side, and why sentence order need not match diagram order. “Read left to right” alone does not teach that. Introduce sufficient as enough and necessary as required through the example, then develop their limits.

For rule versus fact, make the hypothetical explicit: a rule tells us what would follow **if** a condition were true. It does not report that the condition happened. Contrast “Suppose this order is marked ready” with “This order is marked ready” before drawing a conclusion about an actual case. Keep the anchor sentence idiomatic and complete. Emphasis and motion around “if” should reveal that hypothetical relationship, with equally clear static information when motion is reduced.

### Technical names arrive at the useful moment

The source gives a simple relationship before dissecting sufficient and necessary. It explains the contrapositive in ordinary terms before presenting the flip-and-negate shortcut. It explains why symbols help before building the first diagram. The label arrives when there is a concrete idea to attach it to.

This does not mean withholding a definition for suspense. Start with what the topic is. Then give the learner an experience that makes its vocabulary meaningful. A name should settle a distinction the reader has seen.

### Small verbal irregularities create a person

The source sometimes says “little squiggle,” “little bracket cage,” “for real,” or “a bunch of different ways.” It also occasionally uses elevated words such as “ballyhoo” or “cumbersome.” This combination makes the prose human without eliminating precision.

For our material, choose the natural word for the situation. Do not manufacture slang or rare vocabulary to imitate personality. Do not import profanity, dismissive reactions, or author-specific tics. The recognizable quality should be a teacher's thoughtful judgment, not a checklist of eccentric phrases.

The page now runs in this order:

1. **What a contrapositive is.** This section has no example. A rule promises the right condition whenever the left is true, so the left depends on the right. If the right is missing, the left has nothing to stand on. The contrapositive is that second reading. It shows "Left → Right" above "Right is false → Left is false", then says it is the same rule told from the other end, not a new one.
2. **Watch it work.** The raffle rule, then the question "You did not get a bicycle. Could you have won?", then the diagram pair.
3. **How to write it.** Swap the sides, put "not" on both, then read it back in English.
4. **What it is not.** A three-row table: swap only (the reverse), "not" only (the inverse), and both (the contrapositive). Each row says whether the rule guarantees it.
5. **The steps.** Four short numbered steps.
6. **Why it matters.** Two short Priya arguments side by side: one guaranteed, one not. After them comes the lab-badge question.

  ## 1. How the sources say to teach the contrapositive, in order
  
  Sources: `brainstorms/2026-09-30-conditional-curriculum/2026-10-11-contrapositive-and-drill-integration-plan.md` (plan, lines 36-66), `references/lsat-teaching-voice-standard.md:333-339`, and the original HTML lines 234-285.
  
  1. **Place it right after Must arrow.** Plan:21-23 puts the meaning there. The written transformation comes later, after forward translation (plan:54-55).
  2. **Keep the rule on screen.** Law school: "enrolled → student" (plan:38-39).
  3. **Change one fact.** The person is not a student. Ask whether they can be enrolled, and require an attempt before the reveal (plan:40). The HTML does this with its coffee/walking example and a STOP.
  4. **Explain from the missing requirement.** Someone who is not a student cannot meet the requirement, so they cannot be enrolled (plan:41).
  5. **Name it only now.** Proposed copy: "The contrapositive tells us what follows when the necessary condition is false," then the concrete line "If you are not a student, you cannot be enrolled in law school" (plan:42). The plan says this is not user-approved.
  6. **Show both forms** as the same relationship expressed another way, not an extra assumption (plan:43).
  7. **Compare four facts.** Enrolled gives student. Not student rules out enrolled. Student alone settles nothing. Not enrolled alone settles nothing (plan:44).
  8. **Fresh check.** Use a clean requirement example with no modality or timing ambiguity (plan:45).
  9. **Do not use "flip and negate" or symbols first** (plan:49). The voice standard (:337) says the shortcut comes after the reader has a reason to believe the result.
  10. **Formal lesson later** (plan:54-66):
      - Read the original arrow in English, reverse the two complete conditions, then negate both, explaining "not" before the symbol.
      - Read the result back in English.
      - Work the four sign patterns.
      - Correct reversal-only and negation-only mistakes.
      - Explain why drills require writing it and why test day may not.
      - Teach whole-condition negation before compound negation. "Not both" is not "neither."
  11. **Never say the rule "disappears" or "can be ignored."** The source says "ignore the conditional." Voice standard:445-453 and the reference-notes audit (lines 23-24) say to state that this rule plus this fact alone settle nothing.
  12. **Do not teach time or causation from the arrow** (plan:49).

The transcripts are raw ASR, so I normalized "contraositive/contra positive" to "contrapositive" and trimmed filler. Quotes are near-verbatim, not letter-exact. Line numbers refer to the files as they exist on disk. I did not check any transcript against audio.
  
  ## 1. Key quotes
  
  1. "So definitionally contrapositive all it means is logically equivalent... think of it as its evil twin... You only know one thing from every conditional." `students/asia/sessions/2026-08-25-conditional-logic/transcript.md:240-243`

  4. "Sufficient means enough... if the necessary condition does not get met, then the one thing that would have guaranteed us that outcome did not happen, because if it did happen, the outcome would have happened." Same file, line 204. This is his clearest causal-style explanation of why the equivalence holds.
  5. "These are like dance partners. These are the only two equivalent statements." Same file, line 262.
  6. "Every conditional has a partner. They're like interchangeable. Everything else you do not know." `students/sandra/sessions/2026-09-28-conditional-logic-foundations/transcript.md:100`
  7. "There's only one logically equivalent statement. One statement that's equally true." Same file, line 246. Also line 260: "Statement one, statement two. They're logically equivalent... I can't know anything else for sure."
  8. "Contrapositive just means the logically equivalent statement. It's the only other statement." When Charles said it is "a different way of writing the original equation," he answered: "Yeah." `students/charles-moore/transcript-downloads/2026-09-09-charles-moore-notes-by-gemini.md:395,409-413`
  9. "The contrapositive is the logically equivalent statement. So they're identical statements." `students/zeina-borji/sessions/2026-09-25-conditional-foundations/transcript.md:936` (and line 944: "they can be used interchangeably").
  10. "Smart" students suffer here: "you could almost get dumber... I only know one other thing to be true outside what is being stated." `students/ivory-johnson/sessions/2026-09-19-session/transcript.md:179`
  11. "The contrapositive isn't intuitive." Same file, line 207; line 237: "we're actually trained to not think in a strict logical way."
  12. "We don't read right to left. We only read left to right... Just because I meet someone at the train station I can't assume they took the same transportation." `NFWI/Moses - 2025_11_03 18_00 EST - Notes by Gemini.md:431`
  13. "The contrapositive is equally valid. If I say if not conclusion then not premise, that's equally valid too." `students/vinay-kalva/sessions/2026-08-29-vinay-lsat-session/transcript.md:567` (and 495).
  14. "If you didn't understand that the contrapositive is equally valid, that would have made this question way harder." `students/ivory-johnson/sessions/2026-10-03-sufficient-assumption-pathways/transcript.md:285`
  
  ## 2. Handles, phrasings and mental moves
  
  - **Vocabulary for the equivalence.** He uses "logically equivalent," "evil twin," "dance partners," "interchangeable," "equally valid," "synonymous" (Sandra:252) and "a different way of saying the original" (Charles). The usual line: "Every conditional has exactly one partner."
  - **Two ways to get there.**
    - The mechanical route is "flip and negate," in two steps, also called "swap and slash" (Dakota:507-516, Fiorella:255). Put the slash through both ideas.
    - The meaning route works backward from the necessary condition: if the requirement fails, the guarantee never fired. Examples are Iowa/US, NYC/US, breathing/air, the Eden fruit/death rule (Sandra:88-102) and arson (Ivory:195-227).
  - **Activation framing.** "You only know what happens when these two triggers get pulled" (Asia:284). The left side fires the rule, and the contrapositive fires when the right side is denied. "Anything else I say, you don't know" (Moses:501-508).
  - **Brain-off discipline.** "The dumber you are, the easier" (Thaniuska 2026-03-09:646). "Turn your brain off" (Ivory 09-19:269). Intuition is the enemy. Dakota (05-02:468): "when you try to be creative is when you get punished."
  - **Say it in plain English, not symbols.** "Say what the contrapositive would mean in normal English... turning it too much into conditional machine-wise can get in the way" (`NFWI/Sonja - 2025 10 31 18_43 EDT - Notes by Gemini.md:978-980`). He says to use a "personalized if-then."
  - **Diagram to train, not to perform.** "We're diagramming so that we don't have to diagram" (Ivory 09-19:207). Also: "don't force yourself to diagram... it's better to just say the contrapositive" (`NFWI/Meeting started 2025_10_30 20_04 EDT:127-129`).
  - **Negation is not opposition.** Rich becomes "not rich," not "poor," and hot becomes "not hot," not "cold." Negation is "adding a not" (Dakota 05-02:525-537, Dillen:273-281). A double negative turns positive (Sandra:287-289).
  - **Chains.** The contrapositive of a chain: "negate from the point you're asked about, and negate everything prior to it. Nothing after it" (Zeina 10-02:1153-1163, Moses:559, Asia:319-327).
  
  ## 3. Mistakes he warns about
  
  - **Reversal or "read backwards."** Dakota:458 and Moses:431. Also the sufficient/necessary mix-up: Ivory 09-19:227, "I didn't say the only way to be guilty of arson is burning a building down."
  - **Denying the sufficient or affirming the necessary.** "Not Kenyan" yields nothing, and neither does "is African" (Asia:280-284). Sofia, 09-11:1015-1017: "you can't negate the sufficient."
  - **Half-flips.** Amanda negated without swapping, or swapped without negating (09-22:637, 659, 649). Ivory 09-19 teaching-notes: "reversing or negating only one side does not follow."
  - **Stating the "false contrapositive" as bait.** Asia:265 and Dillen:309-317. The true contrapositive often sits in the answer choices looking odd: "they're going to try to bait you with a negation of the law."
  - **Compounds.** For a requirement list, "it just requires one of those to be missing" (Azaria 10-26:619; Sabrina, `corpus/Curriculum/Necessary Assumption/Notes/_scan_Sabrina...EST - Notes by Gemini.md:856`; NFWI Meeting 2025_12_22:1503). He says "and" turns to "or" (the megadoc `_megadoc_notes_conditionals.md:62` states the rule, but its authorship is unverified).
  - **Negating "more."** "Not more" means "at least as" (Kevin 2025-11-29:709-731).
  - **Group 3 and 4 words.** "Never," "cannot," "not both" go to the right side, negated. With "cannot" plus "unless," apply the unless rule once (Zeina teaching-notes).
  - **Which terms to use.** Charles asked about converse and inverse. His answer: "no no just contrapositive" (09-09 downloads:398-399).
  
  ## 4. Link to question types
  
  - **Must be true / inference.** This is the heaviest use: "they're going to test your ability to see the contrapositive applied to the situation." He says it is "almost always" the contrapositive (Sofia 09-11:649-651,679; Azaria:694; Moses:508, "maybe half the questions"; Charles 09-14:351-367). "A fact can trigger the rule or the contrapositive." Contrapositives apply only to rules, not facts (Charles 09-14:353).
  - **Chains.** The answer is "head to tail" (Charles:395-412, Sofia:635, Asia:308). If you panic: "oh, look for the contrapositive" (Charles:399-401). "They might throw the contrapositive as a plot twist" (Sofia 09-23:451).
  - **Sufficient assumption.** A hidden path requires contraposing a link (Ivory 10-03:438-450, 285-287). The bridge may appear in contrapositive form (Vinay:567-587; Sofia 09-25:261). His SA notes say "the correct answer loves to put that connection in contrapositive form" (`corpus/Curriculum/Sufficient Assumption/Notes/_megadoc_notes_sufficient_assumption.md:63`, from "Sufficient Assumption.gdoc"). I could not verify that this gdoc is his own writing.
  - **Necessary assumption.** The tie is thin. Sabrina (Necessary Assumption note, line 191) has him say "the rule they give you, the contrapositive equally applies," so the facts apply to either form. Moses:508 links it to "what is missing from the argument."
  - **Flaw.** He calls it a "false reversal" or "false contrapositive" (Gianna 10-08:657; Mounosha 2026-01-25:135-156).
  
  ## 5. Beyond or against the framing
  
  - **The framing is confirmed.** "If the left is true, the right is true; if the right is false, the left is false" matches his usage almost word for word (Fiorella:192; Thaniuska 03-09:648).
  - **Beyond the framing:**
    - He adds a closed-world claim: "there is only one other thing you know." Everything else is unknown, not false.
    - He adds the activation framing, the "personalized/plain English readback" move, and the "contrapositive as bait in answer choices" point.
    - He adds a practice-versus-test split. Drill with diagrams, then read it in your head.
    - He adds that you should "do the contrapositive up front... it will become subconscious" (NFWI Meeting 2025_12_22:1430).
  - **Looseness or conflicts to be aware of:**
    - "Everything else you don't know by default is wrong" (Fiorella:264) and "Everything else is false" (Charles 09-09:413). Elsewhere he says "unknown." These should not be quoted as "false."
    - "Only one logically equivalent statement" is a teaching simplification. Other equivalent rewrites exist, such as "not A or B."
    - He once calls the contrapositive "the inverse" (Sabrina:762, Meghan 03-17:171), which clashes with standard terminology.
    - "A rule disappears when a fact leaves the other side undecided" is the voice-standard's phrasing, and the project plan says not to claim it.
  - **Third-party material, not Joshua's words:**
    - The Joshua-selected "voice standard" HTML (`raw/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice-standard/conditional_reasoning_transcription.html:254-261, 378-420`) has a coffee/walking thought experiment and the line "the contrapositive is contained within the original statement." Its authorship is unidentified, and it mentions Logic Games.
    - "Conditional intro" handout (`brainstorms/2026-09-30-conditional-curriculum/source-audit-2026-10-11/notes/2025-09-03_Conditional intro.txt:19-50`): "literally the same relationship expressed in reverse form." Authorship unverified.
    - Lecture 33013 and the Loophole SA transcript are other presenters.
  - **Proposed wording that is not Joshua's** (`brainstorms/.../2026-10-11-contrapositive-and-drill-integration-plan.md:45-46`): "It is the same relationship expressed another way, not an additional assumption." The file itself marks this as "proposed wording, not a user-approved quotation."

"What a contrapositive isThe contrapositive is the same rule, said a different way. The two are logically equivalent. If one is true, the other is true. You can use either one. They're interchangeable.Here is what it says: if the right side of a rule didn't happen, the left side didn't happen either.Why? Because if the left side had happened, the right side would have come with it. It didn't come. So the left side didn't happen.One rule, said two waysOriginal. The left side is true, so the right side is true.Left side→Right sideContrapositive. The right side is false, so the left side is false.Right side is false→Left side is falseThat's the one other thing a rule tells you for sure. Anything else you might guess is still unknown.See it workHere is a rule: If you win the raffle, you get a bicycle. Winning is on the left. The bicycle is on the right.You did not get a bicycle. Could you have won the raffle?Yes, you could still have won.No, you could not have won.The rule leaves it open.That's the contrapositive: If you did not get a bicycle, you did not win the raffle. The ~ sign means not.The raffle rule, said two waysOriginalWin the raffle→Get a bicycleContrapositive~Get a bicycle→~Win the raffleSame rule. Same truth. Say it either way.How to write itStart with the original: Win → Bicycle. Two moves turn it into the contrapositive.Swap the sides: Bicycle → Win. Then put “not” on both: ~Bicycle → ~Win.Read it back in plain English. If you did not get a bicycle, you did not win. Does the rule say that? It does. That check catches most mistakes.If a condition already has a “not” in it, negating it takes the “not” away. The practice below covers all four cases.What it is notTwo other moves look like the contrapositive. Each one does only half the work.Three ways to change Win → BicycleWhat you doWhat you getDo you know it's true?Swap the sides only. This is the reverse.Bicycle → WinNo. A bicycle could be a gift.Add “not” only. This is the inverse.~Win → ~BicycleNo. Not winning doesn't mean no bicycle.Swap the sides and add “not” to both. This is the contrapositive.~Bicycle → ~WinYes. It says the same thing as the original.Wrong answer choices use the reverse and the inverse on purpose. Each one is a single move away from the right answer.The stepsWrite the rule as a diagram. The sufficient condition goes on the left. The necessary condition goes on the right.Swap the sides.Put “not” on both conditions.Read it back in plain English. Check it against the rule.Why it mattersThe LSAT often gives you a rule one way and then uses it the other way. You'll see the contrapositive as a conclusion, as an answer choice, or as one link in a chain. If you know it says the same thing as the rule, you spot it right away.Everyone who wins the raffle gets a bicycle. So anyone who didn't get a bicycle didn't win.The second sentence isn't new information. It's the first sentence turned around. A student who doesn't see that treats it as a leap. A student who does sees a rule they already had.Rule: If a visitor enters the lab, the visitor has a badge. Which is the contrapositive?If a visitor has a badge, the visitor enters the lab.If a visitor does not enter the lab, the visitor has no badge.If a visitor has no badge, the visitor does not enter the lab.Write the meaning you already understandWin means you win the raffle. Get bicycle means you get a bicycle. Our original is Win → Get bicycle.We already know that not getting a bicycle rules out winning. Write that as ~Get bicycle → ~Win. The ~ sign means not. Not getting the outcome is different from not having received it yet.To write a contrapositive from a diagram, reverse the two sides and negate both complete conditions. A condition already containing not loses that not when we negate it. Let's try all four patterns.1. Neither side negativeIf you win the raffle, you get a bicycle.W = win the raffle. B = get a bicycle.W → BWhich is the contrapositive of W → B?B → W~B → ~W~W → ~B2. Necessary side negativeIf the machine is testing, its indicator is not lit.T = machine is testing. L = indicator is lit.T → ~LWhich is the contrapositive of T → ~L?L → ~T~L → T~T → L3. Sufficient side negativeIf the café is not closed, its doors are unlocked.C = café is closed. U = doors are unlocked.~C → UWhich is the contrapositive of ~C → U?U → ~CC → ~U~U → C4. Both sides negativeIf an item is not marked fragile, it is not on the glass shelf.F = item is marked fragile. G = item is on the glass shelf.~F → ~GWhich is the contrapositive of ~F → ~G?~G → ~FG → FF → GThe complete five-step routineFind the indicator. Read its job in the whole rule.Identify both complete ideas. Keep restrictions such as current or submitted by Friday.Place the roles. Sufficient goes left; necessary goes right. Choose labels you can read back.Write the contrapositive. Reverse the sides and negate both complete conditions.Read both in English. Compare them with what the original sentence actually requires.The original and its contrapositive describe the same rule. If writing both helps you see that rule, write both. If you can read the contrapositive from the original when it is relevant, you needn't copy it merely to have a second line.Read an arrow forward to use its stated direction. To read its contrapositive, start from the right condition's absence and work back to the left condition's absence. That is a different move from reading the arrow backward unchanged.Negate the whole conditionTo negate a condition, describe what makes that complete claim false. Adding “not” to one convenient word can change the claim.A form is signed and dated. What makes that complete condition false?It is missing a signature, a date, or both.It has neither a signature nor a date.Every covered duty is superseded. What is the negation?No covered duty is superseded.At least one covered duty is not superseded.An evaluator with current authorization signs the form. What makes this false?One evaluator does not sign.No evaluator with current authorization signs.Numbers need the same care. If a condition means “at least three reviewers request revision,” its negation is “fewer than three do.” If it means “exactly three,” the negation is “not exactly three.” Never silently change the numerical meaning.When a condition is long, define a symbol for the whole condition and write NOT with that symbol. Then explain what its falsity means in ordinary words.Practical questions about diagrammingUse writing to make your reasoning easier to checkWhen should I diagram?While learning, write enough to make your choices visible. A difficult sentence, several rules or a connection you keep rereading can be a reason to put the rule on paper. With practice, you may hold a simple rule mentally. Use the writing to help the reasoning.How do I check whether my diagram is right?Read it back and compare the whole meaning. Did you keep each restriction? Does your arrow promise something the original leaves open? When a diagram is wrong, record the exact phrase and the mistake it produced. That gives you something specific to practice. Familiarity alone is not proof of correctness.What if writing takes time?Start by producing a correct, readable diagram. Speed is a later practice goal. Writing can reduce rereading, but it does not save time in every case. Compare how it helps on the rules you are actually working through. First make the rule accurate. Work on speed once you can explain why the arrow follows. Should I always write the contrapositive?You need to understand it every time. Write it when it helps you use the missing requirement, inspect your translation or connect rules. Otherwise, you may read that direction from the original. Extra notation should have a job.Why this practice asks for bothIn the integrated drill, write or choose the original and the contrapositive. We're checking that you can produce both. Once that skill is reliable, a performance task need not require both lines for every rule.How should I abbreviate the conditions?Use short labels that bring back the complete idea. W might mean win this raffle. Current pass may be clearer than a single letter if you're tracking several kinds of pass. Shortening should save writing without losing meaning. There is no fixed letter limit.Only envelopes with a paid stamp go into this tray. Someone wrote Paid stamp → In tray. What needs repairing?The labels need to be longer.The arrow makes the requirement enough.Nothing; the first phrase always goes left.Two possible rooms, one limitLet's take the same reasoning into a sentence whose required condition contains a negative. Accept this rule for the exercise:If a room is showing a film, it is not hosting a workshop.Use F for showing a film and W for hosting a workshop. Our rule is F → ~W. A film requires the absence of a workshop. We haven't said a film is the only reason a room might have no workshop.Given factThis room is not hosting a workshop: ~W.Which possible rooms fit both the rule and that fact? Select every one that fits.WFilm showing. No workshop.WNo film. No workshop.WFilm showing. Workshop underway.Check these possibilitiesThree combinations fit. One breaks the rule.If you win the raffle, you get a bicycle.The rule requires a bicycle when you win. Let's compare all four possible combinations. These are possible cases, not a claim that any of them happened.Possible cases under the raffle ruleWin the raffle?Get a bicycle?Fits the rule?YesYesYes. The required result is present.NoYesYes. The rule allows a bicycle without a win.NoNoYes. With no win, the rule settles nothing about a bicycle.YesNoNo. This is the single violation.A conditional is broken when its sufficient condition is present and its necessary condition is absent. That's why no bicycle rules out a win: a winner without a bicycle would be the forbidden combination."

{"file_path": "C:\\Users\\Joshu\\dev\\ai-os\\wiki\\contrapositive-teaching.md", "content": "---\ntitle: Contrapositive, how Joshua teaches it\ntype: concept\ntags: [lsat-u, conditional-logic, contrapositive, pedagogy]\ncreated: 2026-10-11\nupdated: 2026-10-11\nstatus: active\nsubject: Contrapositive\naliases: [contrapositive, logical equivalent]\n---\n\n# Contrapositive: how Joshua teaches it\n\nGathered 2026-10-11 from his own session transcripts (Gemini auto-notes, raw ASR, so quotes are near-verbatim and unchecked against audio). Use this before writing any contrapositive lesson, drill, flashcard or explanation. Voice rules live in [[teaching-voice]] and [[COPY-CORRECTIONS]]; this page holds the substance.\n\n## The core idea, in his words\n\n- **It is the logical equivalent.** \"Definitionally, contrapositive all it means is logically equivalent.\" The two statements are interchangeable and equally valid. Source: `students/asia/sessions/2026-08-25-conditional-logic/transcript.md:240-265`.\n- **Same statement, said another way.** When a student said it is \"a different way of writing the original,\" he said yes. `students/charles-moore/transcript-downloads/2026-09-09-charles-moore-notes-by-gemini.md:395-413`. Also `students/zeina-borji/sessions/2026-09-25-conditional-foundations/transcript.md:936`: \"they're identical statements.\"\n- **Every conditional has exactly one partner.** His handles: \"evil twin,\" \"dance partners,\" \"interchangeable.\" `students/sandra/sessions/2026-09-28-conditional-logic-foundations/transcript.md:100,246`; `students/_other-sessions/2025-10-30-fiorella-notes-by-gemini.md:262`.\n- **Why it is true, in his reasoning.** Sufficient means enough. If the necessary condition didn't happen, the sufficient one couldn't have, because if it had, the outcome would have happened. `students/_other-sessions/2025-10-30-fiorella-notes-by-gemini.md:204`.\n\n## How he says to use it\n\n- **Two moves.** Flip and negate, also \"swap and slash.\" Put the slash through both ideas. `students/dakota/...:507-516`.\n- **Read it back in plain English.** Say what the contrapositive means in normal English. Turning it too far into symbols can get in the way. `raw/sources/lsat-curriculum/students/New Folder With Items/Sonja - 2025 10 31 18_43 EDT - Notes by Gemini.md:978-980`.\n- **Negation is not opposite.** Rich becomes \"not rich,\" not \"poor.\" Hot becomes \"not hot,\" not \"cold.\" A double negative turns positive.\n- **Diagram to train, not to perform.** \"We're diagramming so that we don't have to diagram.\" Drill with the diagram, then say it in your head. `students/ivory-johnson/sessions/2026-09-19-session/transcript.md:207`.\n- **Do it up front.** It becomes subconscious with practice.\n- **Chains.** Negate from the point you're asked about and negate everything before it, not after.\n\n## What students get wrong\n\n- **Reading right to left.** \"We only read left to right.\" Meeting someone at the train station doesn't mean they took the same transport.\n- **Denying the sufficient or affirming the necessary.** Neither gives a conclusion.\n- **Half the move.** Swapping without negating (reverse) or negating without swapping (inverse).\n- **Bait.** Wrong answers use the reverse or inverse on purpose. The true contrapositive often sits in the choices looking odd.\n- **Compound conditions.** For a requirement list, one missing piece is enough. \"And\" becomes \"or\" when negated. The megadoc states the rule (`corpus/Curriculum/Conditional Logic/Notes/_megadoc_notes_conditionals.md:62`); authorship of that line is unverified.\n- **Negating \"more.\"** \"Not more\" means \"at least as.\"\n\n## Where it shows up on the LSAT\n\n- **Must be true and inference:** he says the contrapositive is \"almost always\" what they test, and facts can trigger the rule or its contrapositive.\n- **Chains:** head to tail, with the contrapositive as the plot twist.\n- **Sufficient assumption:** the correct answer often states the connection in contrapositive form (`corpus/Curriculum/Sufficient Assumption/Notes/_megadoc_notes_sufficient_assumption.md:63`, authorship unverified).\n- **Flaw:** called a false reversal or false contrapositive.\n\n## Cautions when writing\n\n- He says \"everything else is wrong\" or \"false\" in places. Teach it as **unknown**. The rule plus a fact on the other side settles nothing.\n- \"Only one logically equivalent statement\" is a teaching simplification. Other equivalent rewrites exist.\n- He once calls the contrapositive \"the inverse\" in a session (`students/sandra/...`). In the bootcamp the inverse is the half move (negate only). Use the bootcamp meaning.\n- The source HTML's \"ignore the conditional\" and \"all you know\" lines overclaim. Do not copy them.\n\n## Wording that failed\n\nJoshua rejected an explanation built on a rule \"making a promise,\" the right side being something the left \"depends on,\" and a left side with \"nothing to stand on.\" It read as sophistry. The version he wants leads with: the contrapositive is the same rule said a different way, logically equivalent, interchangeable, then says plainly what it states. See [[COPY-CORRECTIONS]], 2026-10-11 contrapositive entry.\n\n## Where it lives in the product\n\n`ai-os-projects/lsatu-conditional-bootcamp/src/bootcamps/conditionals/components/OpeningCourseContent.tsx`, function `ContrapositiveNote`, lesson id `contrapositive`, titled \"Contrapositive\".\n"}

Rules from the two rounds:\n\n- **Say what it is first, in plain words, before any example.** Name the idea, say what it states, then show it. The contrapositive is the **same rule said a different way**. The two are **logically equivalent**: if one is true, the other is true, and you can use either. Lead with that. Source: his own teaching, [[contrapositive-teaching]].\n- **No metaphor-of-obligation language.** Never write that a rule 'makes a promise', that the left side 'depends on' the right, or that it has 'nothing to stand on'. Say what is true and why, in the plainest reasoning: if the left side had happened, the right side would have come with it.\n- **Keep it natural, intuitive and personable.** Contractions, short sentences, a teacher thinking next to the student. No lecture-hall phrasing. If a sentence sounds like it is trying to sound wise, cut it.\n- **Name the lesson by its topic.** 'Contrapositive', not 'Write and read the contrapositive'.\n- **A lesson explains before it drills.** Order for a concept lesson: what it is, see it work, how to do it, what it is not, the steps in short, why it matters. Do not open with an example or a question.\n- **Must arrow note must say:** what the arrow says, where to start reading (the left), and what the arrow is for. Not just which side is sufficient.\n- **A card front asks the question, the back gives the answer first.** The answer is the large line. Be specific: 'Can you read a rule from right to left?', not 'Can you reverse the arrow?'. Never 'the other one'.\n- **Fix the vague word, not the sentence.** 'Reverse' and 'the other one' were both rejected as not specific. Say what the student actually does or what the condition actually is.\n- **Words:** 'Correct', not 'Right', on a correct answer. Bold the word 'if' where the point is that the rule says what follows *if* something is true.\n\nStill to reconcile (found 2026-10-11, not Joshua decisions): 'establish' is banned below but his approved necessary copy uses it; the raffle was approved for the opening and later rejected as the sufficient anchor; see \\\\\\`.claude/skills/bootcamp-builder/references/teaching-preferences.md\\\\\\`.\n\\`;\ns=s.replace(marker,()=>entry);\n// words table additions\nconst row='| \\\"Drill 1\\\" as a title |';\nif(s.includes(row)){ const i=s.indexOf(row); const end=s.indexOf('\\n',i); s=s.slice(0,end+1)+'| \\\"a rule makes a promise\\\", \\\"depends on\\\", \\\"nothing to stand on\\\", \\\"the other one\\\" | say what is true and why, in plain words. Added 2026-10-11: reads as sophistry; vague words like \\\"the other one\\\" point at nothing |\\n| \\\"Right.\\\" on a correct answer | \\\"Correct.\\\" Added 2026-10-11 |\\n'+s.slice(end+1); } else throw new Error('row');\nfs.writeFileSync(p,crlf?s.split('\\n').join('\\r\\n'):s);\n\"\ngrep -n \"Contrapositive and arrow lessons\" wiki/COPY-CORRECTIONS.md | head -2; grep -n \"nothing to stand on\" wiki/COPY-CORRECTIONS.md | head"}

conditional_reasoning_transcription.html
All of those statements in the example column mean the exact same thing as the sufficient family examples. It is the exact same concept expressed in a bunch of different ways. What’s important isn’t how the conditional is expressed, but the core relationship hiding underneath the language. This is why we have a symbolic way of representing conditional relationships, to push past the language and focus only on the relationship between the two variables.
Pay special attention to ONLY IF! It is a necessary indicator, even though it contains the word IF. You can think of it like the ONLY is dominant over a recessive IF.
The Contrapositive
So far, we’ve explored the sufficient condition, the necessary condition, and the relationship between the two. Specifically, we’ve gone over the core of the conditional relationship:
If the sufficient condition occurs, then the necessary condition must occur.
This relationship is ironclad. We’ll call it the Must Relationship. Remember this as we explore a conditional thought experiment.
Imagine you’re a coffee-obsessed individual. If you’re walking, you’re on your way to get a coffee. Getting a coffee is the only reason you ever walk anywhere. Every single time you walk, you are getting a coffee.
Now, imagine that you are not on your way to get a coffee.
What do you know about yourself?
Put your guess here: ______________________________
STOP
Really try to figure this out right now. Don’t read the solution until you have a guess. I put this question to every student. The ones that hazard a guess (either voluntarily or under protest) always come away better off.
If you are not on your way to get a coffee, you haven’t met your necessary burden, right? So could you have activated your sufficient condition? NO.
The fact that you are not on your way to get a coffee means you are not walking.
REMEMBER!
If the necessary condition is absent, the sufficient condition is absent too. This is the contrapositive.
As you’ve seen, the contrapositive can be produced organically. It is not some foreign process that needs to be performed on conditionals. The contrapositive is sitting there within the original conditional, waiting to be recognized. If you have not met your necessary burden, you cannot have activated the sufficient condition.
Let’s return to our original example to see how its contrapositive looks:
If I’m breathing, then air is present.
OK, so if I’m breathing, air is needed. I have to have air for the breathing to happen. The air is completely necessary. What if I don’t have air? That means I don’t have the thing I need. That implies that I couldn’t have activated my conditional relationship. So, I can’t be breathing. No necessary conditions means there’s no sufficient condition.
The original statement and its contrapositive are all you know from your conditional relationship. The LSAT will try to tempt you into erroneously surmising other things from your conditional. Don’t fall for these ploys. For example, they will give you a conditional statement, and then say, “We know the necessary condition is happening!” When they do this, you should be like, “Cool. So I can ignore the conditional.” The presence of the necessary condition doesn’t make anything happen.
Check out this chart showing what does and does not activate your conditional relationship:
    SituationsWhat Has to Happen?If I’m Breathing, Then Air Is Present.
    Sufficient PresentMust have necessary conditionI’m breathing, so there must be air.
    Necessary AbsentCan’t have sufficient conditionThere’s no air, so I can’t breathe.
    Sufficient AbsentNothing!There’s no breathing happening, so we don’t know anything else.
    Necessary PresentNothing!There’s air around, so we don’t know anything else.
The first two rows of this chart represent the only two situations that make anything happen with your conditional relationship. The conditional is just a rule; it takes the right situation to activate it. Literally anything other than those two things can happen and you don’t need to do anything with your conditional. Without the situations in the first two rows of this chart, the conditional might as well not exist.
How to Diagram Conditionals

49:Once you have the original statement written down, you can flip and negate both sides to quickly jot down the contrapositive.
50:I don’t recommend diagramming the contrapositive on every conditional you encounter just for the sake of doing so, but sometimes it will be useful to write the conditional in its contrapositive form, either for clarity or to facilitate a chain. Conditionals can be written either way: as the original statement or as the contrapositive. Both statements are describing the same relationship.

55-Quick Conditional Diagramming FAQ
56-  Q.When should I diagram a conditional?
--
68-    Diagramming does not take nearly as much time as you imagine it does. There is a huge gap between perception and reality when it comes to LR timing. It takes a lot less time to diagram than it does to reread a difficult sentence ad infinitum. Writing things down frees up mental resources for other tasks. You need all the mental power you can get when you’re doing LR, so use the paper to your advantage.
69-    Also, you have to walk before you can run. You’re not going to be fast at first and that’s ok. This book contains strategies designed to help you get faster, but you’re not there yet, and attempting to get there now will only delay your progress toward mastering these concepts.
70:  Q.Should I always diagram the contrapositive?
71-  A.
72:    No. Many people claim that always diagramming the contrapositive saves time, but it actually ends up costing you much more time than it saves, especially on Logic Games. You should understand conditional reasoning on a deep enough level to look at the original conditional diagram and know that the contrapositive is also true (reading the contrapositive instead of writing it). This works for the same reason you knew the contrapositive was true in our initial contrapositive thought experiment. The contrapositive is not a separate statement. It is contained within the original statement. If you can write it, you can already read it, so don’t waste time putting extra noise on the paper.
73:    That said, there are times when diagramming the contrapositive is useful, and you should definitely diagram it if you have a reason to do so. Sometimes you’ll need the contrapositive to facilitate a chain. Just don’t write the contrapositive out of habit and you’ll do great.
74:You’ll diagram the contrapositive in all the drills in this chapter. Why? I want to verify that you have the ability to identify the contrapositive. Once you see that your contrapositives match the drill answer keys, you’ll be more confident simply reading the contrapositive in your head from the original statement.
75-  Q.What’s this abbreviation stuff?
76-  A.
77-    When you diagram, you should shorten each condition to a capital letter or two. For example:

How to Diagram Conditionals
Now that we have a handle on the Must Relationship, let’s take a look at how to represent it symbolically.
  IF — Sufficient Condition→THEN — Necessary Condition
        when(ever)
        any(time)
        all
        every(time)
        in order to
        people who
        each
      →
        must
        necessary
        required
        only (if)
        depends
        need (to)
        have to
        essential
        precondition
The Must Arrow
The conditional relationship is ideal for symbolic representation because it is always the same underneath the varying language. We symbolize the conditional relationship with one simple sign:
Sufficient  →  Necessary
              ↑
          Must Arrow
We use the Must Arrow to signify the Must Relationship. It is a reminder of the urgency of conditionals. Every conditional relationship can be written like this. We call it diagramming the conditional.
Here are your steps for diagramming a conditional relationship:
  See a conditional indicator.
  Draw a Must Arrow.
  Place an abbreviation of the word or phrase targeted by the conditional indicator on the correct side of the Must Arrow.
      Sufficient is on the left, before the Must Arrow.
      Necessary is on the right, after the Must Arrow.
  Fill in the other side of the Must Arrow with whatever is left over in the statement.
Let’s take diagramming conditionals for a spin together. We’ll start with our favorite statement:
If I’m breathing, then air is present.
I see a sufficient indicator in front of breathing, so I’ll place a B in the sufficient condition:
B →
Now, I’ll fill in the other side of the Must Arrow with A for air, since that’s what’s left in the statement.
....
    If I’m a chair, then I’m furniture.
    We would shorten the “chair” to C and shorten the “furniture” to F. Ending up with this diagram:
    C → F
    For more complex conditions, just use the minimum number of letters that you will actually understand. Say one of the conditions is “maximum return on investment,” you should shorten that to MRI or MR… Whichever option calls up the condition in your mind will work. Don’t write out the whole word or more than four letters per condition. The notation becomes cumbersome when you write more than that.

I read the contrapositive section of the source HTML: `raw/sources/conditional-reasoning-voice-standard/conditional_reasoning_transcription.html`, lines 234 to 285, plus its diagramming section and FAQ. No author is named in it.

**How it teaches the contrapositive**
1. **Order.** It comes after sufficient, necessary and the core relationship, which it calls the "Must Relationship" and "ironclad". The student reaches it with the whole rule already in hand.
2. **Experience before the name.** It uses a coffee-and-walking thought experiment, then says "STOP... Put your guess here" and "Don't read the solution until you have a guess." The reveal follows: you haven't met your necessary burden, so could you have activated the sufficient condition? "NO."
3. **The name comes last.** "If the necessary condition is absent, the sufficient condition is absent too. This is the contrapositive."
4. **Equivalence.** This is its strongest line: "It is not some foreign process... sitting there within the original conditional, waiting to be recognized." The FAQ adds "The contrapositive is not a separate statement. It is contained within the original statement." It also says "Both statements are describing the same relationship" and labels the pairs "Equivalent Contrapositive".
5. **Reasoning in first person.** The breathing and air example is a walk-through: "What if I don't have air? That means I don't have the thing I need... so I can't be breathing."
6. **Four-situations chart.** Only two situations make the rule do anything: the sufficient condition present, or the necessary condition absent. The other two tell you nothing.
7. **Notation.** It writes `~A → ~B`, calls the squiggle a "not", and shows four original and contrapositive pairs covering each sign pattern. The method is "flip and negate both sides."
8. **Read it, don't always write it.** "Every conditional can be read two ways": forward, or backward "negating everything as you go". It says not to write the contrapositive out of habit. Write it for clarity or to build a chain. The drills require writing it to prove you can, so that you can later read it in your head.

**Cautions**
- **Overclaims.** It says the rule and its contrapositive are "all you know", and that when the necessary condition is present you can "ignore the conditional". Your voice standard says not to copy those. Say it settles nothing instead.
- **Examples.** Breathing and air is on your do-not-use list, and the source mentions Logic Games.

**Compared with our lesson**
- **Matches:** equivalence, the same relationship, reading it back in plain English, and flip and negate.
- **Missing:**
  - the prediction pause before any reveal;
  - "reading the rule backward while negating everything" as a skill;
  - the line that the contrapositive is contained in the original and waiting to be recognized.
- **"Needn't copy it."** The lines I called contradictory come from this source. Its point is that you must be able to read the contrapositive, not skip it. We can say that more clearly without lowering its importance.

I'd add a short "predict first" pause and the "read it both ways" skill. Do you want both?

## assistant

I rebuilt the contrapositive lesson on the source's method and pushed it as `b58bbdc8`. All 337 conditionals tests and typecheck pass. I tested the card in the preview. I typed a guess, turned it, and the back showed the answer and my guess.

**New order** (what you called out is marked):
1. **Something hiding inside every rule.**
   - **Recap:** it recaps the rule using left side and right side of the arrow.
   - **Card:** a thought-experiment card follows: "Imagine you hate the cold. If your coat is on, you're going outside. Going outside is the only reason you ever put your coat on." Then: "Now imagine you are not going outside. What do you know about your coat?"
   - **Guess box:** there is a "Put your guess here" box and "Try to answer before you turn the card."
   - **Answer side:** turning it shows "Your coat is not on", the reasoning, a "Remember: if the right side of the arrow is missing, the left side is missing too. This is the contrapositive" box, and your guess. A "Turn back" button returns to the front.
   - **After:** "It was sitting inside the original rule, waiting to be recognized." Then logically equivalent, interchangeable, and a diagram pair.
2. **Say it out loud once more.** A first-person walk-through on a ceiling light and its power, then the four-situations table. In two rows the rule settles nothing, and I did not say "ignore the conditional" or "all you know".
3. **How to write it.** The squiggle as "not", four original-and-contrapositive pairs with new letters, and flip and negate in four steps.
4. **What it is not.** The reverse and the inverse, on the light example.
5. **Read it, don't always write it.** Forward, or backward putting "not" on everything. The drills ask you to write it to show you can find it.
6. **Why it matters.** The LSAT turns a rule around, plus the badge question.

There is no mention of Logic Games, breathing and air, coffee, or the raffle in this note. I wrote all the wording myself, so please read it.
